Temp and backup siblings follow the 7.1 writer's grammar, `<target>.<32-lowercase-hex>.tmp` and `<target>.<32-lowercase-hex>.bak`. Because every outbox writer holds `consume.lock`, reconciliation deletes every such sibling it finds there regardless of age; the section 8 reaper never enters `state\outbox\`, and the directory counts as one direct child of `state\` against the reaper's discovery ceilings.

A record is immutable once committed and has exactly these root fields:

```jsonc
{
  "stateEventVersion": 1,
  "logicalOutboxId": "0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef-12",  // <incarnationId>-<sequence in canonical decimal>
  "incarnationId": "0123456789abcdef0123456789abcdef",
  "sequence": 12,
  "kind": "consumed",                 // consumed | snapshot | resync
  "createdAt": "...",                 // canonical UTC o
  "principal": "...",                 // caller-supplied opaque token
  "repairOperationId": null,          // opaque token for kind resync, otherwise null
  "adapterVersion": 2,
  "game": "disco_elysium",
  "baseline": { "generatedAt": "...", "source": { /* exact five-field source */ } },  // or null
  "sourceFloor": { "domainId": "...", "saveFile": "...", "savedAt": "..." },
  "refresh": { "attempted": false, "commitObserved": false, "attempts": 0 },
  "warnings": [],
  "baselineReset": false,
  "diff": { /* complete 7.2 diff */ },
  "state": { /* exact complete 6.1 target digest */ }
}
```

`sourceFloor`, `refresh`, `warnings`, `baselineReset`, `diff`, and `state` have the 7.5 envelope's meaning and pairwise invariants. The record deliberately carries no `cacheDegraded` field, because the non-authoritative cache phase runs after the record is committed; that fact travels in the receipt instead, and `consume-cache-fallback` is therefore never a record warning. `baseline` is the `(generatedAt, source)` identity of the baseline the diff was computed from, or null when no trustworthy baseline existed; reconciliation step 7 reads it to recognize a record whose apply is outstanding. The *target identity* of a record is `(state.generatedAt, state.source)`. Kind invariants are exact: `consumed` requires `baseline` to be null or to differ from the target identity; `snapshot` requires `baseline` to equal the target identity, a canonical empty diff, and `baselineReset: false`; `resync` requires null `baseline`, a canonical empty diff, `baselineReset: true`, and a non-null `repairOperationId`. In every kind `baselineReset` equals `(baseline is null)` and `(diff.baselineGeneratedAt is null)`. `principal` and `repairOperationId` follow the shared opaque-token profile: NFC UTF-8, no C0 or C1 control characters, 1 to 128 Unicode scalars, and at most 512 UTF-8 bytes. The core stores and compares `principal` and never interprets it, which keeps 7.1's rule that the core does not infer playthrough identity.

`Read-StateEventRecord` is the record validator and always materializes through `Read-CompanionJson` with a new budget profile `state-event` whose three caps equal the `turn-context` profile's. Structural mode enforces the exact root field set, every invariant above, file-name and field sequence equality, `logicalOutboxId` composition, a `Read-DiffDocument`-valid diff, and a core-valid state. Producer mode additionally admits `state` through `Assert-AdapterDigestDocument` under the held adapter configuration and requires `adapterVersion`, `game`, and source domain to equal that configuration's. `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` validates a new record in producer mode before commit. Reconciliation validates pending records in structural mode, with one exception: under `-Resync` it uses producer mode, so that a repair also sets aside a record whose adapter version, game, or source domain no longer matches the held configuration. Settlement and the consumer switch resolve no adapter configuration.

Version changes follow from those two modes. A core `schemaVersion` change makes every record written under the old version structurally invalid, because its nested documents no longer validate; every outbox operation then stops at `outbox-record-invalid`. That stop is deliberate and is not a wedge: it is the signal the runtime answers with the `-Resync` repair, which quarantines the stale records and commits one `resync`. An `adapterVersion` change alone leaves old records structurally valid, so ordinary operations continue; the runtime recognizes the change from the version values in the receipt and answers it with the same `-Resync` repair, whose producer-mode reconciliation quarantines those records (runtime feature). Either way a version change is a reset point, never a silent mix of shapes.

`ledger.json` contains exactly integer `outboxLedgerVersion: 1`, 32-lowercase-hex `incarnationId`, nonnegative integer `highWaterSequence`, and array `settled`, and is read with budget `config`. Each `settled` entry is a tombstone with exactly integer `sequence`, string `outcome` (`delivered`, `abandoned`, or `quarantined`), 64-lowercase-hex `recordSha256` (the SHA-256 of the exact record file bytes), string-or-null `logicalOutboxId` and `kind` (null only when the outcome is `quarantined`, whose bytes could not be trusted), string-or-null `journalId` and `eventId` (non-null exactly when the outcome is `delivered`), string-or-null `repairOperationId`, and canonical UTC `o` `settledAt`. Entries are unique and sorted by `sequence`. Every ledger write first raises `highWaterSequence` to at least every sequence it tombstones. The next sequence is always `max(highWaterSequence, highest record-file sequence) + 1`. Tombstones older than 180 days are pruned, then the oldest beyond 512, with two limits: a tombstone whose record file still exists is never pruned, and pruning never lowers `highWaterSequence`. A late settlement for a pruned tombstone is `settlement-unknown`, a terminal answer the runtime records without retry.

Ledger lifecycle: reconciliation creates the ledger when it is absent and rebuilds it when it is content-invalid. A new ledger always gets a fresh `incarnationId` from 16 random bytes, and its `highWaterSequence` is the highest sequence among the record files present, or 0. Surviving record files keep the incarnation they were written under and count as pending. A rebuild is a degraded recovery: settlement evidence is lost, so a survivor that had already been settled is drained and settled again, which the runtime absorbs as a duplicate. It can never reissue an identifier, because new records carry the new incarnation and sequences above every survivor. A rebuild beside surviving record files, or after a content-invalid ledger, adds the receipt warning `outbox-ledger-rebuilt`. A ledger `infrastructure-failure` is never a rebuild trigger; it is `outbox` / `outbox-setup-failed`. Scope: the companion root. Complete writer list: reconciliation, settlement, and the abandon release. Complete reader list: the same three plus sequence allocation in `Publish-StateEvent.ps1`. The runtime never reads the ledger.

Quarantine entries are named `<original record name>.<32-lowercase-hex>.invalid` and are created only by moving a record file's bytes unchanged. The core never reads them again; they are diagnostic evidence, never replay input. The directory keeps at most 64 entries, oldest deleted first.

#### Reconciliation

Every outbox operation runs the same reconciliation immediately after its ownership check and before any other outbox or baseline work. It is the single place where a partially persisted transition is completed, so the order is fixed:

1. Ensure `state\outbox\` exists. Failure is `outbox` / `outbox-setup-failed`.
2. Best-effort delete every temp and backup sibling in `state\outbox\`. A failure adds `pipeline-cleanup-degraded` and continues.
3. Enumerate direct children. More than 1,024 entries is `outbox` / `outbox-setup-failed`.
4. Read the ledger and create or rebuild it per its lifecycle.
5. Finish retired files. Delete each record file whose sequence has a `delivered` or `abandoned` tombstone, and move each record file whose sequence has a `quarantined` tombstone into `quarantine\`. This completes a settlement, an abandon, or a quarantine that crashed after its ledger write. A failure adds `pipeline-cleanup-degraded`; a tombstoned file is never pending.
6. Validate every pending record in structural mode. Outside repair mode, any invalid record is `outbox` / `outbox-record-invalid` and the operation stops with no mutation: the bytes may be the only copy of a consumed transition, so they are never deleted or skipped automatically. In repair mode (a `-Resync` publish, which validates in producer mode, or an `-AbandonUndelivered` release), one ledger write first adds a `quarantined` tombstone for every invalid record, which fences its sequence, and only then is each file moved to `quarantine\`. A crash between that write and a move is completed by step 5 of the next reconciliation, and the fenced sequence is never reissued.
7. Apply. Let H be the highest-sequence pending record. H is *applied* when the baseline reader returns `valid` and the baseline's `(generatedAt, source)` equals H's target identity. H is *outstanding* when the baseline is `valid` and its `(generatedAt, source)` equals H's recorded `baseline` identity, its own predecessor; an outstanding H is never superseded, so it is written below whatever the generation times say. Otherwise H is *superseded* when the baseline is `valid` and its source identity is newer than H's target under 7.1's shared source order, or when the two tie under that order and the baseline's `generatedAt` is later than H's `state.generatedAt`. The tie rule therefore only ever decides a record whose predecessor is no longer the baseline, such as a revived record beside a later digest of the same save. Reconciliation reads the baseline structurally, because settlement and the consumer switch hold no adapter configuration: a baseline written under another adapter version can count as applied or superseded here and still be `content-invalid` to the adapter-admitted read of the publish that follows, which then takes 7.1's reset path. A baseline `infrastructure-failure` is `compute` / `baseline-read-failed`. If H exists and is neither applied nor superseded, write `baseline.json` := H's exact `state`; a failed write is `outbox` / `outbox-apply-failed` and the operation stops.

Step 7 is the roll-forward that makes the record, not the baseline, the commit point. Settlement is accepted only in sequence order (below), so H is normally the newest committed record, and an H that is not applied means the apply after its commit did not complete or the baseline was damaged from outside; rewriting the baseline from H restores "last consumed state equals newest committed record" in both cases. The superseded test keeps 7.3's rule that the baseline never moves backwards. It can be met only when H is not really the newest record, which takes a ledger rebuild that revived an already settled record or a repair that has just quarantined a newer one. H is then left alone, and the next publish computes its diff from the newer baseline. A clock step that inverts the tie rule's `generatedAt` comparison can roll the baseline back to an earlier digest of the same save. Two digests of one save identity are normally produced from the same bytes by the same mapper (a mapper change requires a new `adapterVersion`, section 8), so the next publish then shows a duplicate transition; they differ only if the save's bytes were rewritten under the same name and timestamp.

#### `Publish-StateEvent.ps1 [-Adapter <name>] [-CompanionRoot <path>] -Principal <token> [-RequireRecord | -Resync -RepairOperationId <token>]`

The script is the runtime-role sibling of `Get-TurnContext.ps1`. It resolves and holds one Acquisition configuration snapshot, owns one transaction-wide operational-warning sink, and runs acquisition attempts in-process with the same three-pass acquisition budget, freshness floor, and stage 1 through 5 behavior as 7.5. An invalid `-Principal` is terminal `config` / `principal-invalid`, and an invalid `-RepairOperationId`, `-Resync` without `-RepairOperationId`, `-RepairOperationId` without `-Resync`, or `-Resync` together with `-RequireRecord` is `config` / `repair-operation-invalid`, before any attempt. Stage 6 is replaced by the outbox-mode consuming operation, a sibling of the 7.3 shared operation in `DigestDiff.psm1`: both run the same locked target freeze, baseline read and classification, stale-target guard, diff computation, and validation through shared functions, and differ only in commit behavior. Its steps are:

1. Acquire `consume.lock` with the 7.1 wait; contention is `busy` and a path failure is `lock`, both with lock stage `consume`.
2. Run the authoritative ownership check for role `runtime`, then reconciliation, in repair mode under `-Resync`.
3. Freeze and admit the authoritative `current.json` target and apply the freshness floor exactly as 7.3 does; failures are the same typed `freshness` outcomes and permit reacquisition.
4. Read the baseline and apply 7.3's rules: `infrastructure-failure` is `compute`; a valid baseline newer than the target is `stale-target`, including under `-Resync`.
5. Select the kind. With `-Resync`: if a pending record, or a tombstone of outcome `delivered` or `abandoned`, already carries the same `repairOperationId`, and step 2's reconciliation wrote no `quarantined` tombstone of its own (reconciliation step 6), the outcome is `replayed` with that record's identity and nothing is written; a `quarantined` tombstone never replays, because quarantined bytes are diagnostic evidence and never replay input, so a repair whose resync was quarantined commits a fresh one under the same `repairOperationId` (the quarantined tombstone keeps that identifier for diagnosis); a reconciliation that has just quarantined a record also commits a fresh one under the same identifier, because the record it set aside may hold a consumed transition the runtime has not journaled, and only a `resync` after it discloses that changes may be missing; otherwise the kind is `resync`, the comparison baseline is substituted by the target as for an absent baseline, and `baselineReset` is true. Without `-Resync`: if the baseline is `valid` and its `(generatedAt, source)` equals the target identity, there is no transition, and the outcome is `unchanged` with no write unless `-RequireRecord` is present, in which case the kind is `snapshot`. In every remaining case the kind is `consumed`, with 7.1's substitution and `baselineReset: true` when the baseline is `absent` or `content-invalid`.
6. Enforce the pending bound on ordinary publishes: without `-Resync`, 16 pending records is `outbox` / `outbox-full`. Nothing is lost, because the unconsumed delta remains between baseline and current. A `-Resync` publish is exempt and commits at any pending count, because the runtime's stream repair must commit its resync before it can drain and settle what fills the outbox; resync records stay bounded because each repair operation has at most one live resync beyond those its own reconciliations paid for (step 5 replays a repeat, a quarantined one is retired, not pending, and a fresh one after a new quarantine follows the retirement of at least one pending record, so it never raises the pending count) and the runtime runs one repair at a time, and the 1,024-child ceiling of reconciliation step 3 is the hard stop.
7. Compute and validate the diff as 7.3 step 3, build the record with the next sequence, validate it in producer mode, and serialize it with budget `state-event`. A diff or build failure is `compute` with 7.3's reasons. A serialization failure is `outbox-write:not-consumed` / `record-serialize-failed`.
8. Commit: write the record to a temp sibling and move it to its final name with overwrite disabled, so an existing target fails the commit and is never replaced. The shipped state writer always moves with overwrite enabled or replaces; this create-only mode is new and is used only here. A temp-write failure is `outbox-write:not-consumed` / `record-temp-write-failed` and a move failure is `outbox-write:not-consumed` / `record-commit-failed`. A successful move is the commit point of the whole transition.
9. Apply: for `consumed` and `resync`, write `baseline.json` := the exact target snapshot the record carries. A failure does not fail the transaction: the outcome stays `committed` with `baselineApplied: false` and the receipt warning `outbox-apply-deferred`, step 10 is skipped with `cacheDegraded: true`, and the next reconciliation completes the apply. A `snapshot` writes no baseline and reports `baselineApplied: true`.
10. Refresh the diagnostic cache exactly as 7.3 step 4's second half. The handled fallback adds the receipt warning `consume-cache-fallback`; every other failure sets `cacheDegraded: true`.
11. Release the lock.

The durable states of one transition, and the deterministic resume from each, are:

| State after | Record file | Baseline | Meaning | Resume |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Any failure before step 8's move | Absent | Old | Nothing consumed. | The next transaction recomputes from the old baseline; an orphaned temp is deleted by reconciliation step 2. |
| Step 8 | Committed | Old | Consumed; apply outstanding. | The runtime may already drain the record. Reconciliation step 7 of the next outbox operation writes the baseline before any other work. |
| Step 9 | Committed | New | Consumed and applied. | None needed. |
| Step 10 failure | Committed | New | Consumed; diagnostic cache stale. | The next pipeline or consuming operation repairs the cache (7.1). |

No state can produce a second record for the same transition, because a new record is built only after reconciliation has proved the newest committed record applied. The transaction budget mirrors 7.5: `outbox-write:not-consumed` permits exactly one new in-process attempt, which may select a newer source, and no branch performs a third consuming attempt:

| Attempt | Result | Wrapper action | Further consuming attempt |
|---|---|---|---|
| First | `committed`, `unchanged`, or `replayed` | Emit the receipt and exit 0. | None. |
| First | `outbox-write:not-consumed` | Reduce it to its diagnostic summary and keep only its operational warnings. | Start one new attempt. |
| First | Any other result | Emit its terminal diagnostic and exit 1. | None. |
| Retry | `committed`, `unchanged`, or `replayed` | Emit the receipt and exit 0. | None. |
| Retry | Any other result, including a second `outbox-write:not-consumed` | Emit one terminal diagnostic and exit 1. | None. |

The exit-0 receipt has exactly string `outcome` (`committed`, `unchanged`, or `replayed`); string-or-null `kind`; string-or-null `logicalOutboxId`; integer-or-null `sequence`; object-or-null `target`; integer `schemaVersion`; integer `adapterVersion`; string `game`; Boolean-or-null `baselineApplied`; Boolean `cacheDegraded`; and array `warnings`. `kind`, `logicalOutboxId`, and `sequence` are non-null exactly for `committed` and `replayed`; `baselineApplied` is non-null exactly for `committed`. `target` is the locked target's `{ "generatedAt": <string>, "source": <exact five-field source> }` identity and is non-null exactly for `committed` and `unchanged`; on `unchanged` it is also the baseline's identity, which lets the runtime notice that the state it holds is not the state the core last consumed, for example after the bridge owned consumption for a while. `schemaVersion` is the running core's, and `adapterVersion` and `game` are the held configuration's; the runtime uses them to recognize events written under other versions and to check its configured adapter identity. Receipt `warnings` use the 7.5 entry shape, composite ordering, and uniqueness, and their legal codes are the wrapper's operational subset from 7.5 plus null-detail `consume-cache-fallback`, `outbox-apply-deferred`, and `outbox-ledger-rebuilt`. The compact receipt is capped at 4,096 UTF-8 bytes. Warnings known before commit travel in the record with the 7.5 envelope's legal codes, minus `consume-cache-fallback`.

The exit-1 terminal record of all three scripts is one line `[state-event:<class-token>] <json>`. Its JSON object has the same exact seven fields, caps, and matrix discipline as the 7.5 terminal diagnostic and is validated by `Read-StateEventDiagnostic`, which applies the 7.5 rules over the registry rows below. `consumptionState` is `notConsumed` only for `outbox-write:not-consumed`; `consumed` only for a `delivery` failure that follows a committed record; `unknown` only for `protocol` / `attempt-result-invalid`, as in 7.5; and `none` otherwise. Provenance follows the 7.5 latch for `Publish-StateEvent.ps1`: `config` clears it; `outbox` raised by reconciliation carries the selected identity and the preflight target's generation; any class raised after the locked target freeze carries that final target's identity and generation. The other two scripts select no save, so all three provenance fields of every diagnostic they emit are null. A failed stdout write is `delivery` / `stdout-write-failed` in all three.

| Class | Allowed reasons | Raised by |
|---|---|---|
| `config` (added reasons) | `consumer-not-owner`, `consumer-owner-invalid`, `consumer-owner-unreadable`, `consumer-outbox-pending` | Every consuming caller of both roles, settlement, and, for `consumer-owner-unreadable` only, the consumer switch. |
| `config` (added reasons) | `principal-invalid`, `repair-operation-invalid` | `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` only. |
| `config` (added reasons) | `settlement-argument-invalid` | `Set-StateEventSettlement.ps1` only. |
| `config` (added reasons) | `owner-argument-invalid`, `release-switch-invalid`, `release-decision-required`, `release-pending-records` | `Set-CompanionConsumer.ps1` only. |
| `outbox` | `outbox-setup-failed`, `outbox-record-invalid`, `outbox-apply-failed`, `outbox-full`, `outbox-sequence-exhausted`, `outbox-write-failed` | The three scripts of this section only. |
| `outbox-write:not-consumed` | `record-serialize-failed`, `record-temp-write-failed`, `record-commit-failed` | `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` only. |
| `settlement` | `settlement-unknown`, `settlement-conflict`, `settlement-quarantined`, `settlement-hash-mismatch`, `settlement-out-of-order` | `Set-StateEventSettlement.ps1` only. |

None of the added reasons accepts atoms. The complete class set of each script is closed. `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` emits `config`, `outbox`, `outbox-write:not-consumed`, and 7.5's `busy`, `lock`, `stale-target`, `no-saves`, `stability`, `source`, `dump`, `digest`, `refresh`, `freshness`, `compute`, `protocol`, and `delivery`, with their 7.5 reasons, stages, and automatic behavior. `Set-StateEventSettlement.ps1` emits `config`, `busy`, `lock`, `compute` (only `baseline-read-failed`), `outbox`, `settlement`, `protocol` (only `attempt-result-invalid`), and `delivery`. `Set-CompanionConsumer.ps1` emits `config`, `busy`, `lock`, `compute` (only `baseline-read-failed`), `outbox`, `protocol` (only `attempt-result-invalid`), and `delivery`. For those two, `busy` and `lock` always carry lock stage `consume`, and `config` also covers `companion-root-invalid`. In those two scripts a failure the script cannot classify, which is an implementation fault rather than a documented outcome, is `protocol` / `attempt-result-invalid` with `consumptionState: unknown`, and the runtime treats it like any other unsuccessful result; `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` keeps 7.5's `protocol` rules. `reset-write:not-consumed` and `reset-write:consumed` are bridge-role classes and never occur in this section.

`outbox-write-failed` covers a write an operation cannot continue without: reconciliation's quarantine fencing ledger write, settlement's tombstone write, and the consumer switch's baseline removal, abandon tombstone write, and marker write. The operation stops at that write with every earlier write in place, and each durable state it can leave is one of the crash states above, which a rerun resumes. A failed ledger creation or rebuild write in reconciliation step 4 is `outbox-setup-failed` instead. A record-file delete or quarantine move that fails after its tombstone is written is not a failure: the tombstone already retires the record, `pipeline-cleanup-degraded` is recorded where the script has a warnings channel, and reconciliation step 5 finishes the file later.

The receipt and the diagnostic are status, never the delivery mechanism. The runtime learns what was consumed only from the record files: after every termination of the child, including a crash, an abnormal exit code, or a deadline kill, it drains `state\outbox\` and trusts only the validated records it finds. The diagnostic's consumption state is therefore advisory on this path: even `unknown` is resolved by the drain, where the bridge had to disclose it.

#### `Set-StateEventSettlement.ps1 [-CompanionRoot <path>] -LogicalOutboxId <id> -RecordSha256 <hex> -JournalId <token> -EventId <token>`

A settlement tells the core that the runtime has durably journaled one record, so the core may retire it. Its arguments are checked before the lock is taken: `-LogicalOutboxId` must be 32 lowercase hex, a hyphen, and a canonical decimal from 1 through 9,007,199,254,740,991; `-RecordSha256` must be 64 lowercase hex; and `-JournalId` and `-EventId` must be opaque tokens under the shared profile. A missing or malformed argument is `config` / `settlement-argument-invalid`. The script then acquires `consume.lock`, runs the ownership check for role `runtime` and then reconciliation, and resolves the identifier:

| Outbox state for the identifier | Behavior | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Pending record whose file bytes hash to `-RecordSha256`, with no lower-sequence record pending | Write the `delivered` tombstone in one ledger write, then delete the record file. | Exit 0, outcome `settled`. |
| Pending record with a matching hash while a lower-sequence record is still pending | No mutation. | `settlement` / `settlement-out-of-order`. |
| Pending record with a different hash | No mutation. | `settlement` / `settlement-hash-mismatch`. |
| `quarantined` tombstone at the identifier's sequence whose `recordSha256` equals `-RecordSha256`, whether or not its `logicalOutboxId` could be trusted | No mutation. | `settlement` / `settlement-quarantined`. |
| Tombstone with equal `recordSha256`, `journalId`, and `eventId` and outcome `delivered` | No mutation. | Exit 0, outcome `already-settled`. |
| Tombstone that differs in any of those values, or whose outcome is `abandoned`, or `quarantined` with another `recordSha256` | No mutation. | `settlement` / `settlement-conflict`. |
| Neither a pending record nor a tombstone | No mutation. | `settlement` / `settlement-unknown`. |

The rows are tried in order. A tombstoned file is never pending, so the pending rows never meet a retired record. The `quarantined` row is keyed by the sequence, the identifier's decimal suffix, rather than by the identifier, because a tombstone written for bytes that could not be parsed carries a null `logicalOutboxId`; hash equality makes that key safe across ledger incarnations, since the record bytes embed their incarnation. The row tells the runtime that the core rejected exactly the bytes it journaled, which it then never delivers; every later row is keyed by the identifier, so a `quarantined` tombstone with a null `logicalOutboxId` and another hash answers `settlement-unknown`.

Settlement is accepted only in sequence order. That keeps the invariant reconciliation relies on: while the ledger is intact, the pending records are the newest committed records, so every `delivered` tombstone has a lower sequence than every pending record. `settlement-out-of-order` is retryable: the runtime settles in ascending sequence and repeats the later settlement after the earlier one succeeds. The ledger write precedes the file delete, so the durable states are: record pending with no tombstone (unsettled); tombstone written and file still present (settled; reconciliation step 5 removes the file, and until it succeeds the tombstone is exempt from pruning); tombstone written and file gone (complete). A settlement never resurrects a record. Because reconciliation runs first, a record is never retired before its baseline apply has succeeded or been superseded. The exit-0 stdout value is exactly `{ "outcome": <"settled" or "already-settled">, "logicalOutboxId": <string> }`. Consumed-diff records are historical facts that cannot go stale, so the pattern's deliveryless `terminalStale` outcome is not legal for this family.

#### Runtime read contract

The runtime reads record files directly and nothing else under `state\`. It opens each with read, write, and delete sharing, reads the complete bytes once, and never writes, renames, or deletes anything in the core's state root. It treats every record file it finds as possibly pending and relies on its own journal to recognize one it has already taken. It learns that a record left the outbox only through its own settlement results. It never reads `ledger.json`, `consumer.json`, `baseline.json`, or `current.json`; every question about those is answered by one of the three scripts.

#### Amendments to closed enumerations

This section extends statements that earlier sections make as complete, closed, sole, or exact. The list below comes from a sweep of sections 1 through 12 for every statement that enumerates entry points, callers, consumers, writers, readers, ownership sites, producers, validators, version fields, or reasons in those terms. A statement is affected when a script, file, field, or reason of this section belongs to the enumerated kind. Each affected passage is edited where it stands and names 7.6; statements about the dump tool, the digest mapping, the diff shape, and the walk are unaffected.

- Version field names (section 1): `stateEventVersion`, `consumerVersion`, and `outboxLedgerVersion` join the closed namespace list. Receipts, settlement results, and switch results are unversioned status values, like terminal diagnostics.
- Directory layout and setup (section 3): the three scripts, `consumer.json`, and `outbox\` are listed; `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` ensures both directories and runs the reaper exactly as `Get-TurnContext.ps1` does, while the other two scripts touch only `state\`.
- Budget profiles, producers, and the `Read-CompanionJson` consumer surface (7.1): `state-event` is added with the `turn-context` caps for records; the marker, the ledger, receipts, and results use `config`.
- Exception hierarchy (7.1): unchanged, as stated at the top of this section.
- Diff-cache writers (7.1): the outbox-mode operation refreshes the cache after its apply, like the shared operation.
- `baseline.json` writers and readers (7.1): reconciliation step 7 and commit step 9 write it under runtime ownership, the abandon release deletes it, and reconciliation reads it.
- Structural-validation table (7.1): the two `Get-TurnContext.ps1` rows apply identically to `Publish-StateEvent.ps1`.
- `consume.lock` consumers (7.1): the three scripts of this section join consuming `Get-Diff.ps1` and `Get-TurnContext.ps1`.
- `cycle.lock` consumers (7.1): the refresh path of `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` joins the watcher, one-shot, and turn-context refresh paths.
- `source.domainId` consumers and source-order consumers (7.1): producer-mode record validation reads the domain; the outbox-mode floor and stale-target checks and reconciliation's superseded test use the order, the superseded test with its `generatedAt` tie rule.
- Diff validator ownership (7.2): `Read-StateEventRecord` structurally validates a record's nested diff, as the envelope and walk-result readers do theirs. Section 7.2 is mirrored verbatim in `core/digest-schema.md`, which the documents test group compares, so the mirror carries the same edit.
- Shared consuming operation and `ConsumeResult` tuples (7.3): the bridge-role operation performs the ownership check and gains one tuple, outcome `config` with consumption `none`, null payload, false cache-degraded, non-empty detail, and null target document.
- Bridge wrapper and registries (7.5): the wrapper performs the advisory marker preflight; the four consumer reasons are legal bridge `config` reasons; and the `[config]` stage includes the ownership check.
- Pipeline callers (7.1, 7.5, and section 8): wherever those sections name the turn-context attempt, refresh path, or wrapper as a caller of the pipeline cycle, the work-file reaper, directory setup, the operational-warning sink, or `last-processed.json` bookkeeping, `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` is a caller of the same kind with identical behavior.
- Entry points, configuration snapshots, and profiles (section 8): the three scripts accept `-CompanionRoot` under the same rules; `-Adapter` applies to `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` only; its wrapper transaction is a configuration-snapshot ownership site requesting `Acquisition`; and the other two scripts resolve no adapter configuration.
- State temps (section 8): the reaper's exact target-name list gains `consumer.json`.
- Standing rules, verification, and anti-goals (sections 10, 11, and 12): each carries its 7.6 entry.

## 8. Component: watcher (core)

`Watch-Saves.ps1 [-Adapter <name>] [-IntervalSeconds 30]` `Update-CompanionState.ps1 [-Adapter <name>]`

All six core entry points, and the three 7.6 scripts, also accept `-CompanionRoot <path>`, defaulting to the parent of the script's directory (the companion root above `core\`); it roots the resolution of `adapters\` and, for the entry points that use them, `state\` and `.tmp\` (Get-Diff uses no `.tmp\`, the walk uses no `state\`; section 3), and exists so fixture tests can point an entry point at a scratch companion tree. An explicit value must satisfy `[IO.Path]::IsPathFullyQualified`, resolve through `[IO.Path]::GetFullPath` to an existing directory, and be opened through literal-path APIs; relative values fail rather than inheriting the process working directory.

For all six core entry points (these two, `Get-Diff.ps1`, `Get-ValidatedTurnContext.ps1`, `Get-TurnContext.ps1`, and `Find-LastEventfulSpan.ps1`, 7.4), and for 7.6's `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` (the other two 7.6 scripts take no `-Adapter` and resolve no adapter configuration), `-Adapter` names a directory under `adapters\`; when exactly one adapter directory exists the parameter may be omitted and resolves to it; with several it is required (exit 1 with the candidate list otherwise); with none, exit 1 directing to install an adapter (a fresh checkout is not a silent idle loop). An explicit adapter is a non-empty single directory name: rooted values, either directory separator, `.` and `..` are rejected before lookup. Core enumerates direct child directories of the normalized `<CompanionRoot>\adapters` directory, selects by `StringComparer.OrdinalIgnoreCase`, rejects an ambiguous match, and verifies that the selected directory's normalized parent is that adapters directory under the same comparison. It never resolves adapter input by joining an unchecked relative path. `Get-AdapterConfig -Profile StateCompatibility|Acquisition` owns two closed startup profiles. Both duplicate-safely parse adapter.json, reject any root name outside the complete eleven-member vocabulary below, and require and validate non-empty string `game`, signed Int64 JSON integer `adapterVersion`, fully qualified string `saveDirectory`, non-empty string `saveGlob`, and optional `diffExcludePaths` defaulting to `[]`. Every exclusion entry must be a canonical RFC 6901 JSON Pointer whose first decoded token is `extra`, which contains at least one further token, and which is ordinally unique after canonical encoding (7.2); empty reference tokens are legal. The state profile derives `sourceDomainId` from the configured path and glob without testing directory existence. Both profiles derive the schema-qualified immutable `artifactRoot` and require the fixed adapter digest-validator module beneath it. Resolution validates every non-module rule for the selected profile before acquiring the validator lease defined below. Acquisition builds on the shared non-module result, requires the save directory to exist, requires positive Int64 `maxSourceBytes` strictly below Int64.MaxValue, requires and validates `dumpCommand` and `digestCommand` as arrays of strings with the operational and placeholder rules below, requires nonnegative JSON integers `dumpEntrypointIndex` and `digestEntrypointIndex` that identify an in-range token in the corresponding command, and validates optional `ambientExtraPaths` under the same pointer rules, default `[]`.

Validator loading has one process-local owner in running core. The cache key is the ordinal fully normalized validator-module path, whose immutable release path already contains adapterVersion and core schemaVersion. `Acquire-AdapterValidatorLease` imports the module from the type-owning core-module scope for the class-visibility reason in section 7.1, captures every PowerShell stream, requires the one exact export, and retains the exact returned `PSModuleInfo`, callable, and reference count only after all checks succeed. The first successful acquisition creates the entry; overlapping resolutions increment its count and receive idempotently disposable leases. A provisional lease belongs to `Get-AdapterConfig` until a fully validated snapshot is constructed, then transfers atomically to that snapshot. Any post-acquisition construction failure releases it in `finally`. Releasing a lease decrements the count. At zero, under cache synchronization it invalidates the lease and callable, replaces the entry with an `unloading` tombstone, then removes that exact module through one stream-silent teardown helper. The helper captures and discards every PowerShell stream, catches every exception, and verifies that the exact module instance is no longer loaded. Clean removal deletes the tombstone. Any emitted stream, thrown exception, or still-loaded instance atomically changes it to a process-lifetime poisoned tombstone; later acquisition fails `adapter-release-corrupt` and never reuses the resident callable. Disposal is idempotent and nonthrowing, so teardown cannot replace an already selected success or primary failure. Import, stream, or export rejection uses the same helper on its exact provisional instance before returning `adapter-release-corrupt`; any teardown defect installs the same poison. A different immutable release path creates a separate entry and never refreshes an existing one in place.

The watcher amortizes validator imports across polls of one release. Each default watcher service bundle owns one run-scoped warm hold, created empty with the bundle; custom service bundles without a hold (the test seam) simply forgo amortization. After every successful poll configuration resolution the poll offers the snapshot's validator path to the hold: an empty hold acquires one extra lease on that path; a hold on a different path acquires the new path's lease and then releases the prior one; a hold on the same path is left unchanged. Acquisition failure, including a poisoned entry, leaves the hold unchanged and never fails the poll: the hold is an import-cost optimization, never a gate, and no consumer ever reads a callable through it, so a hold lagging the active release merely retains the prior module until the next successful adoption or the run's end. The watcher run releases the hold exactly once, in terminal cleanup after the poll loop exits for any reason; process exit remains only a backstop. With the hold resident, per-poll snapshot disposal leaves the entry's count above zero, so one release imports once per watcher run rather than once per poll, while an adapter release change swaps the hold and lets the prior release unload through the ordinary zero-count teardown.

Every successful configuration snapshot is disposable. Its complete ownership sites are the watcher poll or cycle, one-shot invocation, `Get-Diff.ps1` invocation, `Get-TurnContext.ps1` wrapper transaction and its short post-lock confirmation snapshot, 7.6's `Publish-StateEvent.ps1` wrapper transaction with the same confirmation snapshot, `Find-LastEventfulSpan.ps1` invocation, and section 10 guidance transaction. Each site releases its snapshot in `finally` after its last validator or policy consumer; a confirmation snapshot is released immediately after comparison. Process exit is only a backstop, not the ordinary release path. A disposed snapshot and callable are invalid and no consumer may retain either.

`Get-Diff.ps1` alone requests `StateCompatibility`; it needs semantic state identity and diff policy but no live acquisition capability. Watcher, one-shot, turn-context, 7.6's state-event publish, and walk request `Acquisition`. Provided adapter.json passes the shared document-level UTF-8, JSON syntax, depth, and duplicate-property checks, a temporarily unavailable save directory or acquisition-invalid, syntactically well-formed unused dump or mapper command therefore cannot block consumption of a valid authoritative state pair, while every entry point that can enumerate or execute fails before doing so. This split was chosen over universal acquisition validation because whole-adapter health would turn an unrelated acquisition outage into loss of already-published state availability. Every profile violation is exit 1 with a stderr message, never a silent idle loop. Only `Find-LastEventfulSpan.ps1` reaches the `ambientExtraPaths` consumers after acquisition validation. `-IntervalSeconds` is an `[int]` (`System.Int32`) parameter. PowerShell 7.5 binding occurs before clamp logic; non-finite or out-of-Int32 inputs fail binding, while successfully bound fractional values use PowerShell's Int32 conversion.

The adapter configuration root vocabulary is exact: it contains only `game`, `adapterVersion`, `saveDirectory`, `saveGlob`, `maxSourceBytes`, `dumpCommand`, `dumpEntrypointIndex`, `digestCommand`, `digestEntrypointIndex`, `diffExcludePaths`, and `ambientExtraPaths`. The state profile requires only its four adapter.json identity members and treats present acquisition-only values as opaque; its derived release root and digest validator are state-compatibility dependencies, not acquisition-only commands. The acquisition profile requires both command arrays and both entrypoint indexes and validates every present optional member. Any other member is a startup configuration error. Closing the vocabulary catches misspelled options before an absent optional field silently selects its default, and it requires every future extension to name its consumers, validation, and compatibility behavior instead of being ignored by older core code. Every successful resolution also computes lowercase SHA-256 `configReceipt` over the exact strict UTF-8 adapter.json bytes that passed the selected profile. It is conservative: whitespace or property-order edits change the receipt and may abort an in-flight publisher rather than allowing mixed configuration. Changing `diffExcludePaths` changes the meaning and representation of ordinary diffs and therefore requires an `adapterVersion` bump. `ambientExtraPaths` changes only the guidance-phase progress classification and may change without a version bump. The returned validated `AdapterCompatibilityConfig` adds the core-derived `sourceDomainId`, `configReceipt`, `artifactRoot`, adapter digest-validator path, and loaded callable plus its validator lease; `AdapterConfig` extends that validated value with acquisition fields and the core-derived `walkPolicyId`. None of these derived properties is an adapter.json member. A receipt is immutable for its snapshot, never persisted or serialized, and is consumed only by the post-`cycle.lock` confirmation gate.

Both profiles derive fully qualified `artifactRoot` as `<adapter directory>\releases\<canonical adapterVersion decimal>\schema-<core schemaVersion>`. Acquisition requires both command templates to reference `{artifactRoot}`. A trusted installed release is a companion-shaped package: its mapper lives at `{artifactRoot}\adapters\<adapter>\Build-Digest.ps1`, its imported module at `{artifactRoot}\core\DigestDiff.psm1`, its independent semantic validator at `{artifactRoot}\adapters\<adapter>\Validate-Digest.psm1`, and its adapter-owned executable and vendored data beside the packaged mapper. The package author must keep every semantics-bearing adapter-owned dependency inside that root and make packaged code resolve relative dependencies there. Core validates the held root and command-template grammar, but it does not inspect arbitrary child code or claim to constrain every file the child may open. System runtimes and the installed package are trusted at this boundary; strict filesystem confinement would require an operating-system sandbox and is outside this design.

`DigestDiff.psm1` is deliberately release-local rather than imported from live companion core. The mapper runs later as a child process, so a live-core edit with unchanged adapter.json would otherwise change an already-confirmed operation outside `configReceipt`. Running core remains the structural, admission, and provenance authority through post-digest validation; the packaged core copy freezes only mapper-side helper behavior for the held immutable release. The adapter validator is separately imported by running core from that same release and never through the mapper's release-local module graph.

Installed release directories are immutable. Acquisition never builds, publishes, repairs, reaps, or deletes them; installation and upgrade are offline operator-owned prerequisites. This specification therefore does not define an online publisher, staging lifecycle, crash recovery, or concurrent release arbitration. It requires only that adapter.json activate a complete trusted release and that an active release never change in place. The source-tree paths in sections 5 and 6 are authoring inputs, while acquisition executes their packaged copies. A missing root or member is installation corruption, not an invitation to patch the active release.

Any command-template, runnable artifact, vendored-data, or adapter-owned dependency change outside a core-schema lockstep upgrade requires a new adapterVersion and release; formatting-only adapter.json edits do not. A separate release identifier or content hash with an unchanged adapterVersion is rejected because core cannot prove that changed code or data preserves digest semantics. The conservative token deliberately prefers a baseline reset over comparing outputs from unproven-equivalent adapter releases. A core-only schema upgrade instead selects a new schema-qualified child under the same adapterVersion, preserving the ownership split in 6.1. The held AdapterConfig continues to name its old immutable release if adapter.json changes after post-lock confirmation, and a complete walk holds one artifact root for every pair. Old-release removal is a manual maintenance operation permitted only after every watcher and companion command using that adapter has stopped.

`adapterVersion` is an opaque equality token, not an ordered counter. Core never compares versions by magnitude, so the complete signed Int64 domain is accepted. In this document "bump" means changing the token, not necessarily incrementing it.

Native Int32 conversion for `MaxSaves`, `MaxAgeHours`, and `IntervalSeconds` is intentional: these are transient current-process operator budgets, and neither their original token nor normalized value enters durable identity or provenance. Mapper receipt and version arguments cross a command boundary and are stamped into durable state, so their canonical decimal grammar remains deliberately stricter. `-IntervalSeconds` below 2 is clamped to 2 with a stderr note (the write-stability gate needs 2-second sample spacing). Get-Diff reads `diffExcludePaths`, `adapterVersion`, and `game` plus derived `sourceDomainId` from its held compatibility snapshot and the state files. Get-TurnContext holds a complete acquisition snapshot and invokes commands only when freshness requires a pipeline cycle.

The watcher never passes the accepted interval directly to `Start-Sleep` or one `Task.Delay`. One watcher-owned `Wait-WatcherInterval -IntervalSeconds <Int32> -CancellationToken <token>` helper stores the remaining seconds in Int64 and waits in chunks of at most 86,400 seconds through the cancellation token's wait handle. It never multiplies the complete interval into Int32 milliseconds. Cancellation during any chunk returns the typed graceful-stop signal; otherwise the helper subtracts the completed chunk and continues until the full interval elapses. Every ordinary end-of-poll idle wait uses this helper. The one-day chunk is deliberately below both PowerShell sleep and .NET timer ceilings, preserving the accepted Int32.MaxValue domain without a runtime-specific upper clamp.

Operational command checks belong to acquisition validation. Each command array contains at least one element, and element zero, the executable token, is non-empty. Empty later argv elements are permitted because an adapter may intentionally pass an empty value. Placeholder coverage and brace validation below still apply to every token, so the command-array requirement is explicit even though an empty array would also fail placeholder coverage.

The watcher exists because the user plays with the game running and wants state to be current whenever they alt-tab to a Companion session; the one-shot path alone would push a manual update step into every conversation. Polling was chosen over FileSystemWatcher deliberately: FSW on this workload brings mid-write events, duplicate and coalesced notifications, and silent handle drops, while a 30-second poll is ample for a save cadence of minutes and trivially robust. Because the diff is always computed against the frozen baseline (7.1), a watcher that misses intermediate saves and a watcher that processes every save produce identical diffs; the watcher is a freshness convenience, not a correctness requirement.

Cycle trigger (watcher only): poll the save glob; "newest" means maximum `LastWriteTimeUtc`, ties broken by ordinal file-name descending (a deterministic pick). Run one cycle when ANY of these hold: the resolved domain plus newest file's `(name, LastWriteTimeUtc)` pair differs from the full `(domainId, name, LastWriteTimeUtc)` tuple stored IN `last-processed.json` (7.1's predicate; the bookkeeping file's own timestamps are irrelevant); that bookkeeping is absent or unreadable per 7.1; or `current.json`'s source identity precedes the newest file's source identity under 7.1's shared order, where an absent, unparseable, identity-incomplete, or VERSION-STALE `current.json` (mismatched `schemaVersion`/`adapterVersion`/`game` versus the running core and adapter) SATISFIES the condition, the same treated-as-absent set the commit guard uses (a failed or stale read is not a clean check; the cycle's step 4 self-heals it, and the version member means the watcher heals an adapterVersion bump on its next poll without any manual step). A narrow prefilter result that would otherwise let the watcher remain quiescent must then adapter-admit those same frozen current bytes. Adapter rejection also satisfies the trigger with a caution. Validator infrastructure failure follows the unavailable-byte rule below and skips the poll. The prefilter avoids unnecessary full validation only when another trigger already requires a cycle; it is never authority to declare current state healthy. This condition is what heals a commit-guard regression regardless of what the bookkeeping says; without it, an adversarial step 6 ordering across concurrent runs could leave a regressed `current.json` behind up-to-date bookkeeping forever. An infrastructure failure while opening or reading `current.json` is not one of those self-healing trigger values: the watcher emits its caution, clears the in-memory stability sample, skips the poll, and retries the read later, preserving the distinction between invalid bytes and unavailable bytes. The one-shot and turn-context bridge skip this trigger gate entirely and always select the newest save: forcing a refresh is their purpose, and the documented recovery path for a corrupt `current.json` depends on an ungated route. All three pipeline callers apply the stability gate and the commit guard inside the serialized cycle publication protocol. Zero glob matches is a normal pre-first-save condition for the watcher (keep polling), exit 1 for the one-shot (`[no-saves]` / `no-matching-saves`), and `[no-saves]` for the turn-context bridge.

Every save-candidate enumeration uses only direct child files of `saveDirectory`, never recurses, and applies `saveGlob` to `FileInfo.Name` through `System.Management.Automation.WildcardPattern` with `IgnoreCase`. The pattern therefore uses PowerShell wildcard syntax, including bracket expressions; it is not a regex or a filesystem-provider path filter. Candidate ordering then follows 7.1's ordinal tie rule.

Write-stability gate (watcher, one-shot, and turn-context bridge; `Find-LastEventfulSpan.ps1` applies this same gate to its newest candidate, 7.4): before copying, the candidate file's size must be identical in two samples taken at least 2 seconds apart. Sample lifecycle: the sample is in-memory state of the running process, keyed to the candidate's `(domainId, name, LastWriteTimeUtc, size)` tuple, with domain and name compared ordinally and timestamp and size compared exactly; it is created when a candidate is first observed, and a candidate is never dispatched on its first observation (including the first poll after process start, so a restart never skips the gate). A changed domain, timestamp, size, or newest file restarts the sequence. The watcher's samples are consecutive polls (interval clamped to >= 2s above). Every poll either records the one complete tuple it successfully observed, dispatches after an identical pending tuple and clears it, or clears the sample when no complete tuple is obtained. The clearing case includes zero glob matches, enumeration failure, candidate metadata failure, and any other exception before the gate observation completes. Thus two matching tuples separated by an empty or failed poll are never consecutive. The one-shot and turn-context bridge sample twice in-process with a 2-second wait, retrying up to 5 times. If unstable, the watcher retries next poll, the one-shot exits 1, and the bridge uses its 7.5 reacquisition budget. Dispatching a cycle CLEARS the sample: a later observation of the same tuple starts a fresh two-sample sequence. A rewrite that changes LastWriteTimeUtc while preserving name and size therefore cannot ride a stale sample past the gate. A rewrite that preserves all three sampled metadata values remains outside this heuristic and is caught, when observable, by the source-receipt protocol. The gate's sufficiency is a game-engine behavior the repo cannot settle; probed in section 11.

adapter.json is re-read and re-validated with the acquisition profile at the start of EVERY POLL, before the glob enumeration, not only at process start: a mid-run edit must flow into the very next poll, and per-cycle would not suffice because `saveDirectory` and `saveGlob` are consumed by the poll gate itself (a corrected glob typo must be able to un-wedge a watcher that has never run a cycle). A re-read that fails validation logs to stderr, clears any in-memory stability sample, skips this poll, and keeps polling with nothing cached as valid (a failed config read is not a clean config). A successful re-read whose derived domain differs from the sample also clears it before candidate observation, so a new directory can never inherit the old directory's first sample.

Cycle (watcher, one-shot, and turn-context bridge refreshes): each cycle invocation passes the `work-cycle` stem to the shared `Invoke-SaveDigest` helper from 7.4. The helper allocates `work-cycle-<32-lowercase-hex>-save.zip` / `-dump.json` / `-digest.json` plus `work-cycle-<32-lowercase-hex>.lease` directly in the `.tmp\` root under the complete nonce, no-overwrite, fresh-mtime, ownership, cleanup, and reaper lifecycle defined there. Steps 1-3 are that shared helper:

The caller acquires `cycle.lock` under 7.1's lifecycle before step 1. It then rereads adapter.json with the acquisition profile and requires the new `configReceipt` to equal the held snapshot before candidate re-enumeration or source acceptance. It never replaces the held object. Read, parse, or validation failure is `adapter-config-confirmation-failed`; a valid unequal receipt is `adapter-config-changed`. In either case the watcher abandons the poll and begins its next poll from a fresh snapshot, while one-shot and turn-context exit `[config]`; no cycle is invoked, no work or state file changes, and the lock is released. Candidate re-enumeration and gate revalidation occur only after successful confirmation and before source acceptance. After that revalidation and before allocating the cycle's work group, the caller runs the full `Invoke-WorkFileReaper`. Its deduplicated typed warning result joins the cycle warning sink. This steady-state pass lets one uninterrupted watcher age out child-private or failed-cleanup orphans rather than waiting for another process startup. `Invoke-CompanionCycle` therefore assumes the caller owns the lock and never opens it recursively. Every terminal path releases it only after finally-scoped cleanup.

The one-shot's post-lock re-enumeration and gate form a closed pre-cycle boundary. An empty successful enumeration maps to `[no-saves]` / `no-matching-saves`; enumeration failure maps to `[source]` / `save-enumeration-failed`; a vanished or unreadable gate candidate maps to `[source]` / `source-vanished` or `source-unreadable`; and a complete false stability result maps to `[stability]` / `stability-exhausted`. Each exits 1, invokes no cycle, publishes no current, cache, or bookkeeping change, leaves baseline untouched, performs only applicable best-effort cleanup, and releases `cycle.lock` in `finally`.

1. Run 7.4's complete metadata, SHA-256, copy, SHA-256, source re-hash, and metadata protocol. A vanished original is source disposition `vanished`; changed name, timestamp, length, or content hash is `changed`; other open, metadata, hash, or copy failures are `unreadable`. Preserve the returned `SaveDigestResult.sourceIdentity` for ordering and `.sourceReceipt` for every later provenance decision.
2. Run the adapter's dump command on the copy (adapter.json `dumpCommand`).
3. Run the adapter's digest command (adapter.json `digestCommand`) writing to the run's digest temp path. `Invoke-SaveDigest` reads and adapter-admits it, then checks all four stamped byte-receipt fields against the returned verified source receipt. Any mismatch is a permanent digest failure before commit.
4. Commit guard, then commit: a narrow read may identify an existing `state/current.json` as a possible strictly newer same-domain candidate. Before that result can discard the run, freeze those exact bytes and pass complete `Read-AdapterDigestDocument` admission under the held configuration. Only when the admitted current source identity is strictly newer than the returned observed source identity under 7.1's shared order does core discard the run's digest with typed `pipeline-guard-discarded` (a slower concurrent run must not regress state) and skip step 5 (the baseline must never be reset to a discarded digest). An existing `current.json` that is absent with a typed file-not-found result, unparseable, core-invalid, adapter-invalid, domain-mismatched, or VERSION-STALE is treated as absent (commit proceeds and returns typed `pipeline-current-self-healed`). An open, access, sharing, device, stream-read, or other infrastructure failure instead returns `failurePhase: commit`, preserves the existing current, and skips cache and bookkeeping. The guard protects freshness only between fully admitted digests. A stale-format or adapter-invalid identity envelope must never win against an admitted predecessor. Without full admission, deleting the newest save while its current digest retained a valid-looking newer identity but lacked required state would wedge the heal loop: the one-shot's reprocess of the predecessor would be guard-discarded as "older" forever. Otherwise move the digest into place as `state/current.json` (the cycle's commit point, atomic per 7.1). The mapper-stamped identity is equal by validation but is never the source of truth for this guard.
5. Run the core differ (`DigestDiff.psm1`) to rewrite `diff-since-last-read.json` from `baseline.json`. A valid baseline is compared normally. An absent or content-invalid baseline writes the canonical empty diff with typed `pipeline-baseline-absent` or `pipeline-baseline-content-invalid`. A baseline infrastructure failure also writes the canonical empty diff and returns `pipeline-baseline-unreadable`. Watcher and one-shot render these notes to stderr; turn-context retains only the infrastructure warning because the later `baselineReset` field carries ordinary absent or invalid baseline state. All three fallback paths leave baseline untouched; the derived cache is allowed to fail closed because the next successful pipeline or consuming operation repairs it. Every `diff-exclude-path-unused` warning produced by this diff computation is appended to the cycle's typed warning sink with its configured pointer detail. A successful fallback sets `cacheWritten` just like a normal cache write. Failure to serialize, temp-write, or replace that fallback produces the ordinary `failurePhase: cache` outcome. The pipeline differ is never a baseline writer. Unexpected adapter-validator infrastructure failure while admitting the baseline or producing this cache does not take a content-invalid fallback: it returns `failurePhase: cache` with internal `cycle-cache` reason `adapter-validator-infrastructure-failed`, leaves baseline untouched, and follows the existing bridge revalidation rule.
6. Update `last-processed.json` from the returned observed source identity, never by rereading mapper-stamped provenance. This step runs on guard-discarded cycles too, so the same save is not reprocessed every poll.

Work-file deletion is not a numbered outcome phase. One finally-scoped cleanup runs after every committed, guard-discarded, and failed cycle and disposes the owned `SaveDigestResult`, deleting only that run's known work paths, tolerating ones already gone such as the digest temp consumed by the commit move, then releasing and deleting the run's lease. A pre-result helper failure already performed the equivalent cleanup. The cycle passes its own warning sink into the helper, and both helper-owned cleanup and result disposal append to that same sink before the cycle outcome freezes `warnings`. Mapper-local cleanup owns any private sibling nonce it created. Every deletion is best-effort: failure returns a typed cleanup warning but never changes the cycle outcome, flags, exit code, retryability, or bridge mapping. The watcher and one-shot render it to stderr; the turn-context bridge routes it through the transaction operational-warning sink into the selected envelope or terminal diagnostic. Startup and per-cycle reaping remain the crash and failed-delete backstop.

`Invoke-CompanionCycle` reports every expected non-cancellation completion through one outcome with exactly these fields:

```jsonc
{
  "completed": true,
  "currentCommitted": true,
  "guardDiscarded": false,
  "cacheWritten": true,
  "bookkeepingWritten": true,
  "failurePhase": null,
  "sourceDisposition": "stable",
  "retryable": false,
  "detail": null,
  "warnings": []
}
```

`failurePhase` is exactly null, `initialize`, `source`, `dump`, `digest`, `commit`, `cache`, or `bookkeeping`. `sourceDisposition` is exactly `stable`, `vanished`, `changed`, `unreadable`, or `not-checked`. `warnings` uses the envelope warning record shape and permits exactly `pipeline-baseline-absent`, `pipeline-baseline-content-invalid`, `pipeline-baseline-unreadable`, `pipeline-cleanup-degraded`, `pipeline-reaper-discovery-limit-exceeded`, `pipeline-guard-discarded`, `pipeline-current-self-healed`, and `diff-exclude-path-unused`. The diff warning requires its configured JSON Pointer; reaper discovery exhaustion requires `tmp` or `state`; other pipeline codes require null detail. A code is present exactly when its named event occurred; cleanup may append its warning after any terminal path without changing the other fields. Watcher and one-shot render the returned warnings. Turn context routes cleanup and reaper warnings through the transaction operational-warning sink and merges remaining attempt-local warnings into a provisional envelope when one remains viable. The following table is the complete legal terminal state space for the nine outcome fields other than `warnings`. `detail` is null on success and otherwise is a non-empty registry string valid for the class to which 7.5 maps that row, except that cache and bookkeeping rows use the explicit internal `cycle-cache` and `cycle-bookkeeping` owners. Free-form exception or child-process text is never stored here. Every tuple not listed is invalid and maps fail-safe to a non-retryable `[refresh]` error at the bridge boundary.

| Terminal path | completed | currentCommitted | guardDiscarded | cacheWritten | bookkeepingWritten | failurePhase | sourceDisposition | retryable | detail |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|---|---:|---|
| Committed success | true | true | false | true | true | null | stable | false | null |
| Guard-discard success | true | false | true | false | true | null | stable | false | null |
| Initialization failure | false | false | false | false | false | initialize | not-checked | false | detail |
| Source vanished | false | false | false | false | false | source | vanished | true | detail |
| Source changed | false | false | false | false | false | source | changed | true | detail |
| Source unreadable | false | false | false | false | false | source | unreadable | false | detail |
| Dump failure | false | false | false | false | false | dump | stable | false | detail |
| Digest failure | false | false | false | false | false | digest | stable | false | detail |
| Commit failure | false | false | false | false | false | commit | stable | false | detail |
| Cache failure after commit | false | true | false | false | false | cache | stable | false | detail |
| Bookkeeping failure after commit | false | true | false | true | false | bookkeeping | stable | false | detail |
| Bookkeeping failure after guard discard | false | false | true | false | false | bookkeeping | stable | false | detail |

Programmer errors outside the expected stages are caught at the nearest stage and use that row's exact flags. They never escape as an untyped success or generic Boolean.

The watcher logs failed outcomes and continues polling. The one-shot maps any failed outcome to exit 1. The turn-context bridge maps phase and disposition through 7.5's complete table; when a post-commit cache or bookkeeping failure leaves a target meeting its floor, it continues with a structured envelope warning and the outcome's preserved flags.

The unique work prefixes isolate debris, while `cycle.lock` serializes source acceptance, publication, cache refresh, and bookkeeping across watcher, one-shot, and turn-context refreshes. The commit guard still rejects a selected source whose identity became older than an already published current digest, but it is no longer an unlocked arbitration mechanism. In particular, once an explicit one-shot returns after mapping replacement bytes at an equal source identity, no cycle that accepted the prior receipt can publish after it: such a cycle either completed before the one-shot acquired the lock or has not yet accepted its source. The watcher trigger remains the recovery path for ordinary strictly older current state, not for equal-identity publication races. Section 10's standing rule can request a bridge refresh while the watcher owns the cycle lock; the bridge waits under the bounded lock rule, revalidates after acquisition, and consumes only after releasing the cycle lock.

The watcher owns one cancellation token from process start through exit. Its idle wait, stability delay, bounded lock wait, `Invoke-SaveDigest` copy and hash loops, adapter runner, and derived-state writers receive that token; one-shot and turn-context callers pass the non-cancelled default. Poll configuration reads, directory existence checks, direct-child enumeration, and candidate metadata reads use synchronous filesystem APIs without a cancellation-token surface. The watcher checks immediately before and after each such call, but a blocked operating-system call can delay shutdown; the graceful interruption bound begins once that call returns. Chunked file work checks between bounded chunks, and the watcher checks before each poll stage, each command, and each publication stage. Cancellation observed at a non-command checkpoint or selected by the command runner's terminal-cause gate bypasses the ordinary poll exception boundary and produces no poll-failure or adapter-failure diagnostic. An ordinary command result selected before a later cancellation remains eligible for its normal diagnostic before the next checkpoint observes the cancellation. If cancellation is observed before the atomic `current.json` move begins, the candidate is not published. Once that move begins it runs to success or its ordinary commit failure without an interruption checkpoint; cancellation observed afterward preserves any committed current and skips every derived cache or bookkeeping stage not yet started. Cancellation during an already-started derived write removes its owned unpublished temp when possible and preserves any completed atomic replacement. Every path then runs work cleanup, releases the lease and `cycle.lock`, and exits 0. This boundary keeps current authoritative and lets the next startup repair skipped or interrupted derived state. Watcher-owned cancellation propagates as a typed control-flow signal through `Invoke-CompanionCycle` cleanup and returns no cycle outcome, including when current or a derived replacement already committed. It therefore does not manufacture an otherwise illegal cache or bookkeeping failure tuple and produces no cycle diagnostic. The internal signal has exactly one payload member, `warnings`, using the same sorted, ordinally deduplicated warning array contract as a cycle outcome. The cycle catches the cancellation request, completes result disposal and releases `cycle.lock`, freezes its warning sink into that signal, and only then propagates it. The watcher's graceful-stop handler renders those cautions before exit 0. Cleanup degradation is therefore visible without reclassifying cancellation as a poll, adapter, or cycle failure.

Failure handling: every poll iteration, INCLUDING the glob enumeration and gate reads that precede a cycle, runs inside the loop's exception boundary, so no failure ever kills the watcher's poll loop except the watcher-owned cancellation signal, which follows the graceful-stop lifecycle above (the same survival guarantee 7.1 gives the poll-gate read). A vanished `saveDirectory` is caught by the per-poll adapter.json re-validation above (its existence check fails, the poll is skipped with a stderr warning, and the loop keeps polling; the directory may return or the config may be repointed), so `Get-ChildItem` never silently reads a missing path as the normal zero-matches state. Each adapter command gets the composite 120-second deadline and bounded teardown above; a process or inherited pipe that outlives it is treated as a command failure (a hung adapter must not stall the watcher silently). A non-zero exit, an exception, or a promised output file missing after a command exited 0 (core verifies `{dump}` exists after the dump command and `{digest}` after the digest command; exit 0 without the file is a command failure) aborts the cycle at that point: later numbered steps are skipped, INCLUDING bookkeeping step 6 on a step 4 or 5 failure, so the cycle retries next poll; no state file changes before the commit point; stderr gets the reason; finally-scoped best-effort cleanup still runs. Additionally, after the digest command, `Invoke-SaveDigest` structurally validates the fresh digest and checks its schemaVersion, adapterVersion, ordinal game, and complete source receipt against core, held config, and the verified observation. Any mismatch is a permanent `[digest]` cycle failure and the digest does not commit. Placeholder substitution is not proof that an external or miswired mapper honored any supplied value. A hard-crash window between steps 4 and 6 leaves `current.json` committed but the cache or bookkeeping stale; both are self-healing derived state. The cache is non-authoritative per 7.1. An absent or different bookkeeping tuple can cause one redundant reprocess, while an equal-identity forced refresh needs none because `last-processed.json` stores no receipt. Work files that survive a hard crash are reaped at watcher, one-shot, `Get-TurnContext.ps1`, and `Find-LastEventfulSpan.ps1` startup (`Get-Diff.ps1` does not reap; the walk reaps the `.tmp\` half only through `Invoke-WorkFileReaper -TmpOnly`, preserving 7.4's never-touches-`state\` invariant). Canonical lease names are exactly `work-cycle-<32-lowercase-hex>.lease` and `work-walk-<nonnegative-canonical-decimal>-<32-lowercase-hex>.lease`; their group prefix is the lease name without `.lease` plus `-`. Each root has independent inclusive discovery ceilings of 65,536 direct-child entries and 16,777,216 cumulative strict UTF-8 filename bytes. Enumeration is lazy and charges an entry before filtering, metadata, classification, or retention. On the first excess, the reaper returns the deduplicated `pipeline-reaper-discovery-limit-exceeded` warning with detail `tmp` or `state`, stops that root, discards its partial index, performs no deletion or lease probe in that root, and continues the other root with fresh counters. Processing a retained prefix is forbidden because a canonical lease beyond the ceiling could prove that an earlier apparent orphan belongs to a live group. After one complete within-budget root enumeration, the reaper indexes canonical leases and assigns every matching member to its group before making any deletion decision. A canonical group becomes age-eligible when its lease `LastWriteTimeUtc` is older than one hour. The reaper then performs the exclusive-open liveness check from 7.4 and either skips the live group or deletes every direct-child file whose name begins with that group prefix, plus the lease last. This includes dump-tool and mapper sibling temps whose second nonce is private to the child. An aged lowercase `work-` direct-child file that belongs to no canonical lease group is an unleased orphan and is deleted individually; directories, case variants, and every other `.tmp\` file are preserved.

The full reaper also deletes orphaned state temps older than one hour. A state temp matches exactly one lowercase target name from `current.json`, `baseline.json`, `diff-since-last-read.json`, `last-processed.json`, or 7.6's `consumer.json`, followed by `.`, exactly 32 lowercase hexadecimal nonce characters, and lowercase `.tmp`. Selection enumerates direct child files only, so the reaper never enters `state\outbox\`; 7.6 reconciliation deletes temps there under `consume.lock`. Nothing else is ever touched (`.tmp\` may hold long-lived scratch such as repo clones).

`Invoke-WorkFileReaper` never writes a process stream. Each root enumeration is an independent best-effort operation. An enumeration failure returns one deduplicated `pipeline-cleanup-degraded` warning, skips that root, and continues with the other root. Each candidate metadata read and canonical lease probe is likewise best effort: failure returns that warning and continues. Metadata or lease-probe failure for any canonical member skips the whole group; metadata failure for an unleased candidate skips only that entry. Deletion failure returns the same warning and continues through remaining candidates. Sharing violation on an exclusive canonical lease probe is the one non-warning case because it positively identifies a live owner. Once each synchronous root operation returns, reaper failure or discovery excess never prevents later startup stages. The watcher, one-shot, and walk entry scripts render the returned warning to stderr. The turn-context attempt routes it through the wrapper-owned transaction operational-warning sink, which carries it in the selected envelope or terminal diagnostic. Discovery excess is persistent until the named companion-owned root falls below both ceilings. The host tells the operator to stop all companion processes, inspect the named `.tmp` or `state` root, safely reduce its direct-child count or filename-byte load, and rerun. Automatic deletion from a partially indexed root remains forbidden. The watcher never touches `SaveGames\` except reading and copying. Exit codes: the watcher runs until interrupted and exits 0 after the graceful interruption lifecycle above. Explicit waits, chunked work, publication checkpoints, and command teardown are bounded as stated; synchronous filesystem calls may delay cancellation observation. The one-shot exits 0 on a completed committed or guard-discarded outcome and 1 on any failed outcome, so the calling session can distinguish them.

`adapter.json` (read by core; the game-agnostic seam):

```jsonc
{
  "game": "disco_elysium",
  "adapterVersion": 2,
  "saveDirectory": "C:\\Users\\asten\\AppData\\LocalLow\\ZAUM Studio\\Disco Elysium\\SaveGames",
  "saveGlob": "*.ntwtf.zip",
  "maxSourceBytes": 1073741824,
  "dumpCommand":   ["{artifactRoot}/adapters/disco_elysium/dump-tool/dedump.exe", "{save}", "-variables-only", "-o", "{dump}"],
  "dumpEntrypointIndex": 0,
  "digestCommand": ["pwsh", "-NoProfile", "-File", "{artifactRoot}/adapters/disco_elysium/Build-Digest.ps1",
                     "-DumpPath", "{dump}", "-SourceDomainId", "{sourceDomainId}",
                     "-SourceName", "{saveName}",
                     "-SavedAt", "{savedAt}", "-SourceLength", "{sourceLength}",
                     "-SourceSha256", "{sourceSha256}",
                     "-AdapterVersion", "{adapterVersion}",
                     "-OutPath", "{digest}"],
  "digestEntrypointIndex": 3,
  "diffExcludePaths": ["/extra/whiteChecks"],
  "ambientExtraPaths": ["/extra/dayPhase"]
}
```

Contract: both commands are argv arrays. Core spawns them with the ADAPTER'S DIRECTORY as the child working directory, substitutes placeholders with absolute paths, and redirects both child stdout and stderr. Adapter commands communicate only through their promised output files and typed process facts, so child text can neither corrupt an entry point's stdout contract nor enter diagnostics. Immediately after process start, the runner starts one asynchronous fixed 65,536-byte `BaseStream.ReadAsync` pump per pipe and writes each chunk directly to `Stream.Null`; it never constructs or retains a complete child-output string or tail. The two pumps run concurrently with the bounded command deadline. The 120-second deadline covers the composite of immediate-process exit and both pumps reaching clean EOF; an exited parent does not end the deadline while an inherited descendant pipe remains open. Child exit and promised-output checks occur only after that complete composite succeeds.
